ParlaMint-DK_20211221131634	Does anyone wish to speak? Sir. Jep Bruus, Social Democracy.
ParlaMint-DK_20211221131654	Thank you. The yoke of responsibility as a parliamentary politician can sometimes be heavy. We make many important decisions, and we must never take the decisions we make lightly. Today, the yoke of responsibility feels extra heavy. We must decide whether one of our fellow Members is worthy to sit here in the Folketing. We must do so because we have a constitutional responsibility to assess whether we have been punished for an act which, in general reputation, makes a member of parliament unworthy of being a member of the Folketing, in accordance with Article 30 of the Constitution. Of course, we must take that responsibility on board. I do not believe that anyone in this Chamber entered politics with a desire to declare a fellow parliamentarian unworthy of his place in the Folketing, and certainly not a colleague who has been a member of the Folketing for 20 years, and one who is, moreover, a well-liked colleague for many of us. But in a case like this, we have to lean on both the law and the court, in this case the Court of Justice, and soberly relate to the specific case. The Court's ruling against Mrs Inger Støjberg could not be much clearer: 25 out of 26 members of the Court of Justice, including 13 Supreme Court judges, have found Mrs Inger Støjberg guilty of the case. The sentence is 60 days of unconditional imprisonment. When you look at history, there is no clear precedent for the decision we have to take. However, if we are to be true to the spirit of the Constitution's provision of dignity, then the conclusion here must be that Mrs Inger Støjberg's ruling at the Court of Justice is incompatible with being a member of the Folketing. A Social Democratic Parliamentary Group agreed on this. I'm sorry it had to come here. Now we are doing our constitutional duty. We take a position on the matter with the seriousness and seriousness that it requires and with due respect for the main character, knowing that it is heavy for all of us who have to make this decision, but of course it is most serious and heavy for Mrs Inger Støjberg to have to bear the consequences of this decision. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20211221134828	Thank you, President. Following the ruling of the Court of Justice against Mrs Inger Støjberg made last Monday 13 December 2021, it is clear that, unfortunately, I would say, an illegal decision was taken, and unfortunately this was done as a result of illegal administration. The Court of Justice has ruled that these unlawful acts were the result of the decision of Mrs Inger Støjberg, who was and is therefore also her responsibility. It is a judgment with which 25 out of 26 judges in the Court of Justice agree, and it is a panel of judges comprising 13 Supreme Court judges, and on that basis the Court of Justice has sentenced Mrs Inger Støjberg to 60 days' unconditional imprisonment. It is a serious judgment, it is also a very harsh judgment, but it is also a very clear judgment, and it is on the basis of this judgment that in my party, the Left, we have discussed the issue of eligibility in our parliamentary group, and that is what we have to take a position on today in the resolution. It follows from the Constitution that the Folketing must take a position, and we have just had that discussion, but we will gladly take it again if anyone so wishes, but it is clear from the Constitution that this is the way it is. Paragraph 30 of the Constitution reads: "Optional to the Folketing is anyone who has the right to vote to do so unless he is punished for an act which in general reputation renders him unworthy of being a member of the Folketing. In respect of the Constitution, we have taken a position, and in the Left it is a unanimous parliamentary group that considers that it is incompatible with the office of a member of parliament to have received an unconditional prison sentence. It is also incompatible to serve while you are a member of parliament. Moreover, the fact that Mrs Inger Støjberg is losing her right to stand as a candidate as a result of an unconditional prison sentence follows the practice followed in previous similar cases, with respect for the fact that we have not seen an impeachment case like this one. The memorandum and summary of votes on the loss of eligibility in previous cases from 14 December 2021 by the Parliamentary Secretariat show that it has been done in similar cases in previous cases where a member of parliament has been sentenced to unconditional imprisonment, for there the Folketing has, however, with a slightly varying majority, but with a typically very large majority, decided that he was not worthy to sit in the Folketing any longer. We have therefore lost our right to stand for election. Moreover, it also follows from the legislation which not only a broad majority of the Folketing, but a unanimous Parliament adopted in 2018, that when municipal politicians and regional politicians were sentenced to unconditional prison sentences, the eligibility of municipal and regional councils is lost. In conclusion, I should like to mention here from the platform that the position of the Left is that if Mrs Inger Støjberg, after she has given her sentence, ended her term of office, re-election and election to the Folketing, it is our party's position, the position of our present parliamentary group, that there is nothing to prevent her from rejoining the Folketing. ParlaMint-DK_20211221144323	Thank you. In SF's parliamentary group, we have also taken a position on this matter. We do not believe that former Foreign Minister Mrs Inger Løjberg can sit in the Folketing with a two-month sentence of unconditional imprisonment. For it is the case that 25 out of 26 judges of impeachment have arrived at, it has been proved beyond reasonable doubt that Mrs Inger Løjberg has deliberately, knowingly and deliberately and despite clear warnings, caused a state administration to break the law and violate citizens' rights. I think there is one thing in this matter that was a little uplifting, because I think that the debate that preceded us suggested that this was about politics. There were probably some who thought that Mrs Inger Løjberg would not be punished fairly because she was a politically appointed judge of impeachment, but in fact it is now the case that, despite this, it is a virtually unanimous judgment of impeachment across political divisions which has reached the same result in relation to the issue of guilt. The relationship that led to judgment seems to us to be a breach of good Danish values of democracy, respect for the law and citizens' rights. What has been the intention in this regard is subordinate, because what counts is the breach of the law and as a minister we must ensure that the law is observed. SF's decision to declare Mrs Inger Løbberg unworthy of the Folketing rests on several elements. Firstly, there is a precedent for losing one's right to stand for election by unconditional punishment, but for example. Not by a fine. This is also apparent from the report on the Mogens Glistrup case of 1983. As Mrs Inger Løjberg has been sentenced to a custodial sentence, it must obviously be the case that, in this case too, she is losing her right to stand as a candidate. Secondly, we in the Danish Parliament in 2018, and it was backed by a whole Parliament, made a number of rules for local politicians, which means that they lose their right to stand for election when they are sentenced to a sentence of liberty. It would be quite strange that we should not make the same claim in the Folketing and say that other rules apply to parliamentary politicians. Could it have been different? Well, no one can really tell that because it didn't end any other way. Now it is the case that it is Mrs Inger Løjberg who is being convicted in this case, but I am sure that several people have a responsibility in this matter, because there was a large majority who kept their hands on this matter, and there is no doubt that when the case ended with a commission and an impeachment, it is also a question of the possibility of getting the matter properly clarified, since a bourgeois majority had prevented it for a number of years. ParlaMint-DK_20211221150437	The issue we are dealing with today is not about political disagreements, even though they exist to such an extent within the room in which we sit. The case we are dealing with today is all about what the Constitution requires the Folketing to deal with in such a serious situation as the one we are in. Paragraph 30 of the Constitution makes it clear that every person is eligible for election to the Folketing unless he or she is punished for an act which is in general reputation makes him or her unworthy of being a member of the Folketing. So today we are dealing with a case in which a member of parliament has been convicted in the Court of Justice for deliberately breaking the law. We need to take this seriously. After thorough discussions in the Enhedslisten Parliamentary Group, we have come to the conclusion that Mrs Inger Løjberg is not worthy to remain in Parliament after the very, very clear ruling of the Court of First Instance last week. In principle, this should be a matter between the electorate and Mrs Inger Løjberg, but in the 2019 parliamentary elections the voters did not know that a commission and two independent lawyers would come to the conclusion that Mrs Inger Løjberg had deliberately broken the law. The voters also did not know that the Court would sentence Mrs Inger Løjberg to 60 days' unconditional imprisonment. It is important for me to say that there is no automaticity in these matters, and therefore it is important that we take this matter seriously and turn every page, every argument, and we have done so in the Enhedslisten Parliamentary Group. Actually, I wish we hadn't gotten here. If the right had stood guard over fundamental democratic principles, Mrs Inger Løjberg had already been overturned when, as Minister, she forced through an illegal instruction and then lied to Parliament about it. But even after more than 200 committee issues, 6 consultations and a very persistent work of Enhedslisten's former MP Mrs Johanne Schmidt-Nielsen was Left, Conservative, Danish People's Party and Liberal Alliance still ready to hold hands over a minister who broke the law. It is only because the blue majority failed to fulfil its responsibilities that, immediately after the parliamentary elections in 2019, we had to set up the Instructor's Commission and then the Court of Justice. I'm actually quite glad we did, so we finally got to the bottom of this case, and so we could finally put a stop. Now we are here with a verdict from the Court of Justice, and it is important for the Enhedslisten that two very thick lines are put in place that it is completely unacceptable for a minister to break the law deliberately and then lie to the Danish Parliament about it. It is no secret that this has not been an easy decision for the Enhedslisten Parliamentary Group. Because regardless of what we think of Mrs Inger Løjberg's positions, it is quite publicly known that Enhedslisten and Mrs Inger Løjberg are politically very disagreeable. Mrs Inger Løjberg is elected by the people, and we are very sceptical about the provision of the Constitution that allows anyone other than the electorate to throw elected representatives out of the Folketing. But it is quite unacceptable and, above all, unworthy that an elected minister should be elected! And it was to the great detriment of the people it was about. We believe in the Enhedslisten that it contributes to the seriousness and indignity of Mrs Inger Løbberg, after she has been sentenced to an unconditional sentence of liberty for abusing one of the most powerful positions in this country, stands and says that she regrets nothing and that she would do it again. If the voters want Mrs Inger Løjberg to be elected to the Folketing again and Mrs Inger Løjberg wants to stand, then the voters can vote for her there, but until the next election we take on board the responsibility that the Constitution imposes on us in the Enhedslisten. When you as a minister have been convicted of deliberately breaking Danish legislation, you have gone so far beyond the limits of what ministers and elected representatives in a democratic society can afford. That is why we in the Enhedslisten Parliamentary Group believe that Mrs Inger Løjberg is not worthy to sit in the Folketing. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20211221154207	Thank you. Thank you to the rapporteur. There are no more brief comments. Sir. Ole Birk Olesen, Liberal Alliance.
ParlaMint-DK_20211221154228	Thank you. I would like to begin by saying a few words about the difference between being a member of parliament and being a minister. There's a huge difference. Although it is often the case that members of parliament can also become ministers and that ministers are most often members of parliament, there is still a huge difference in being a member of parliament and being a minister. Members of parliament make the laws, and the ministers exercise power on the basis of the laws adopted by the Folketing. It is part of the division of power that we know in Denmark. That's completely formal. But there are also some other differences. If, for example, there is one... Some sort of bullshit in the press, members of the opposition are often very quick to say: the minister has to do something about that! And those members of parliament can often be quite indifferent to whether the law allows the Minister to do one thing or the other or the third. They may also be indifferent to the existence of law of gravity and limited financial resources. The minister just has to solve this problem, says members of parliament, especially when they are in opposition. It's not something I've made up, and I'm very sorry that it's like that. I think that we in the Liberal Alliance, both when we are in opposition and when we have been ministers for a short time, try to be the same and only say that things must be done if that is possible. However, other parties that have ambitions to be popular parties and attract many voters tend that when they are in opposition, they demand anything from the minister and say that the minister is a huge idiot if he cannot make it happen. The Minister, on the other hand, is bound by the fact that the things that the Minister can do must be in a law. The Minister must also find the money for it, and the Minister cannot suddenly conjure up 100 engineers who can create a signal program on the railroad if these 100 engineers do not exist. So the Minister has another task, and it is sometimes going out to take the beating that it gives when trying to explain the context, trying to explain that what the opposition demands without thought is not possible because there is a law that prevents it, or because you do not have the money for it, or because there are not enough engineers in the kingdom who can create railways. It is really useless to be a minister in those situations because you know that when you stand on television, people sit out there and don't understand what you're just saying, and you just think, as the opposition says, you're a huge idiot because you don't make that happen. But you do it because it belongs to the ministry. Then we come to this case of child bride in Denmark. At some point it is clear to the public and also to the Danish Parliament that these couples come to Denmark, and that this is not in line with the way we perceive things in Denmark, if you get married at 14 and then have to be with a 10 or 15-year-old man and start having children at 15 and all sorts of things. That is why it must be stopped, and everyone agrees on that. However, we have legislation which is based both on international law and also on our own administrative law, which says that those between 15 and 18 years of age can only be separated on an individual basis. The couples in which one party is under 15 years of age can be separated immediately because they are under the age of sexually low age, and if it is a marriage, one must assume that something happens in the marriage that usually happens in marriages so that they can divorce immediately. But they are between 15 and 18 years old, that is. Over the age of sexually low age, but during the age of majority, you can only separate them by individual assessment. That's what the Folketing wants. The parliament demands: Stop it there! Stop that! What the Minister should have done in this area is the ministerial work that I have been talking about here was going out to the public and being willing to take the beating that comes from explaining the context, because it is one's job as minister. Mrs Inger Løjberg did not want to take on the task of minister in that situation. She did not want to go out to take the beatings that came from explaining the context that it is not possible because the law gives these 15-18-year-olds the right not to divorce their spouse without an individual assessment. Unfortunately, as in so many other cases, Mrs Inger Løbberg was much more concerned with her popularity than with doing what had to be done in this matter. And that is why Mrs Inger Løjberg dared not say to the public that this was not possible. That is why Mrs Inger Løjberg chose to say to her ministry, who warned her: make this happen. The Ministry said it can't be done, it'll be illegal. Mrs Inger Løjberg said: make it happen. That is what Mrs Inger Løjberg has been convicted of in the Court of Justice, namely that she said: make it happen, even though she was told it was legal. And Mrs Inger Løjberg did so because she would not take on the task of the Minister and explain the matter to the public because it would go beyond her popularity and because Inger Løjberg cares infinitely about her popularity. So that's why it's ended up here. Mrs Inger Løjberg has been convicted by impeachment because she did not want to be the minister, but would rather be popular. And that's a serious sentence. It is a judgment which means that Mrs Inger Nøjberg is going to jail for two months for a serious crime, because it is a serious crime when a minister acts in violation of the law, knowing that action is being taken in violation of the law. It's a serious crime. As a minister, the division of power in Denmark, where the Danish Parliament makes the laws, and the government carries out the administration and exercises power on the basis of the laws. Mrs Inger Løjberg has gone beyond that and instead just broken the law. It's a serious crime. I can hardly imagine any more serious crime than a minister who does not care in a parliamentary context, in relation to our democracy, with the fact that the law which the Minister is set to administer and exercise power on the basis of is broken by an order from the Minister. That is why the sentence is relatively harsh. It's not just a suspended sentence, it's an unconditional penalty. Mrs Inger Løjberg is going to prison, alternatively wear an ankle bracelet if she applies for it. That is why I have no doubt that Mrs Inger Løjberg must be declared unworthy of sitting in the Folketing. This is because we have a constitution that says that the Folketing must take a position on it. And it must be the Folketing, because in cases such as this, where the voters were not aware that there would be a verdict in the Court of Justice after the elections in 2019, some others must take a position on the dignity of the MP after the MP has been convicted. Would the Left have set up Mrs Inger Løjberg at all if Mrs Inger Løjberg had received an impeachment before the elections in 2019? Would Mrs Inger Løjberg even stand on the ballot paper at the Left if Mrs Inger Løjberg had been convicted before the 2019 elections? I doubt it. Would there be voters who would not have voted for Mrs Inger Løjberg if they had known that Mrs Inger Løjberg had violated the law and would be convicted in the Court of Justice? Yes, I think there would be voters who would not have voted for Mrs Inger Løjberg on that basis. And that is why we must now declare Mrs Inger Løjberg not worthy to sit in the Folketing. Let me also say a few things to the parties who say and have said that Mrs Inger Løbberg did exactly the right thing; that was unfortunately just illegal. This also applies to the Social Democracy: no, because if the Social Democracy meant it, the Social Democracy should have taken the initiative to change the law. Social democracy has not done so, and I do not think we should do so either. For let us now take some of the couples who were separated without any individual assessment, and there was for example. a 17-year-old woman and a 18-year-old man. Well, in Denmark we don't have the tradition that 17-year-olds and 18-year-olds get married, but we do have the tradition that many 17- and 18-year-olds live together as if they were married. This means that we are not talking about them as a child bride, because there is a 17-year-old and an 18-year-old who sleeps in the same bed at night, it is not child bride and if they had got married, as was the tradition of old times in Denmark, we would not call them child bride either. I just went to the stats before I came up here. Before 2016, there were on average seven couples per year in the previous 10 years, Danish couples who were married, although one was under 18 years old, by special permission from the municipality, namely what we in the old days called a royal letter. So we are also doing this here in Denmark, and that is why it requires an individual assessment in those cases with those couples, and that is why I am not in favour of abolishing the individual assessment. I also do not understand why social democracy should think it should be done, and I do not understand why the Social Democrats do not propose that this should happen. So I actually think that this is just a piece of talk, where the Social Democrats also want to keep on the popular side of the line, and that is why we are also voting here for the indignity of Mrs Inger Nøjberg.

ParlaMint-DK_20211221173659	Thank you, President. I'm really sorry to be here today. I also know it's been a long day, so I'll try to be as brief as I can. It will probably be difficult to make the ultra-card, because we are in this absolutely absurd situation, that the Minister for Foreign Affairs and Integration will not answer the most basic questions of the Folketing about what kind of applicants we are about to grant Danish citizenship here in a moment. Nor will the Minister refer the applicants' cases to the Committee on the Rights of the Child when we ask for it. In my view, this is completely unheard of, also from a historical perspective, and a fundamental break in the way we have granted Danish citizenship over the last 170 years. It is according to the Constitution that the Danish Parliament grants Danish citizenship, not the Minister for Foreign Affairs and Integration. We just have a little difficulty with that here in the Danish Parliament, if it is the case that the Minister does not want to work with the Committee on Bodily Rights. I would like to give an example just to show how absurd the situation is: I have even asked the Minister if there are any people on this bill that we will be third-reading shortly, which has previously been a threat to Denmark's security. Even the Minister will not answer that question. In addition, we have a very good agreement, we think, from April with among others. Social democracy, where we made a new agreement on citizenship. The agreement states clearly: "The granting of Danish citizenship is not a right. I mean, just because you live up to the circular, you don't have the right to citizenship. But if it is the case that the Minister does not want to pass a single case on to the committee, then it will be a right, thereby breaking the Social Democratic Party with the agreement that they themselves have agreed to. Our agreement also states that an applicant and now I quote "not committed crime." Without going into detail for the sake of the people who are on the bill, I would just like to say overall that there are people who have convictions for theft, sentences for heeling, sentences for shoplifting. There are very many of them, it has apparently become popular sentences for illegal work, convictions for illegal residence and drug convictions. The list is really, really long with fines up to over DKK 350,000. So these are sentences below prison level, but I suppose there are sometimes up to five sentences on the same person. We in the Conservatives do not believe this to be true of the wording of our agreement that no crime has been committed. Nevertheless, in an attempt to work with the committee and the Folketing on our part, we have said that if we in the committee can vote against those who we do not want Danish citizenship in confidence, then we would actually like to vote green for the bill in this Chamber. Even the Minister will not allow that; he blocks to have just one case transferred to the Committee on Bodily Rights. It is people from around the world who have come here, have taken Denmark to heart, have embraced our values and often also have a Danish girlfriend or a Danish spouse. We say to you clearly: Yes, you must have Danish citizenship. But then there is, among other things. the criminals here on the bill, i.e. the ones I mentioned before. They have not embraced Danish values. We will not grant them Danish citizenship. To them we say no. But precisely the vote that we would have liked to have had individually on the Committee on the Rights of the Child blocks the Minister, and that is why we unfortunately see no other way but to vote yellow now in the Folketing. I would like to conclude by thanking my colleagues in the blue bloc, in particular Morten, Marie Krarup and Mette Thiesen. Thank you for the support in the many consultations and in the questions and other matters that we have had with the Minister. We parties do not see who should have Danish citizenship in the same way, but what we blue parties see in the same way is that there must be transparency in the committee, and we blue parties have had a great cooperation on that. So I know that on the day of the return of a blue majority, there will actually be transparency in the committee when the Minister will respond to the questions to which the Committee on Bodily Rights wishes to respond. Finally, I would like to call on the Minister to do the same in relation to the next proposal on citizenship in 2022 by changing course and actually to start working against Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20211221182207	We all have in our drawer below our table a fine little book, Denmark's Kingdoms Constitution. You can look up on page 23, and it says in section 44: "No foreigner can have citizenship without law. This means that it is this Parliament Hall that decides who should have granted the Danish citizenship. You may agree with or disagree with that, but that is the way it is. Then there is reference to Grundtvig and history, and it is also true that if you go back in history, you can see that here in the Folketing you actually had an individual case for each foreigner who sought Danish citizenship because it was so few. There were some years when it was 10 or 15 foreigners who gained citizenship. Last year the Folketing here announced citizenship to nearly 5,000 foreigners. This is because something has happened from Grundtvig's time, in fact only until a few decades ago and so far, the obvious fact that Denmark has received hundreds of thousands of immigrants in a few decades. Again: One may agree with or disagree with that, but those people are now staying in our country. By the way, we have an alien law today which grants tens of thousands of residence permits to foreigners every year forever. This year we have granted more than 10,000 residence permits for foreign workers who come here on our business schemes. They bring married couples, and sometimes they bring children. We have granted residence permits to thousands of students coming to our country and to thousands of family members. We are a country that writes tens of thousands of residence permits every eternally single year. So there are some people who come to our country and there are also plenty of Danes who migrate from our country and who move to London, to Paris, to New York, to Australia, to China or to other places. This is the way our world is screwed up, and as a parliament, like Parliament, we have to deal with the fact that some of the people who migrate to our country, who constitute zero integration problems, who are a huge financial boost and who come here to strengthen Danish business or because they have fallen in love with a Dane, actually want to stay in our country. They may have taken an education in Denmark. They have paid it themselves at our universities, they have not received a penny in su, and they have found a job afterwards. They want to stay in our country and we have to deal with the fact that they want to stay. Some of them are granted permanent residence permits and, after a while, have taken our country so much to heart that they also want to seek citizenship in our country. Under what conditions should they be able to obtain that citizenship? Yes, we can point out in our little book on section 44 that they must submit an application and that then we will be 179 people who decide on each individual application 5,000 times a year. You can do that. There are some bureaucratic, administrative and practical problems with this, which I think are obvious to all of us, but I also think there are some political problems. I think it should be the case that foreigners living in this country should be able to access a website, and then they should be able to read some conditions and know that if they live up to those conditions, a majority of the Danish Parliament has said in advance that they will press the green button when the bill on citizenship notification is to be dealt with thirdly. I think they should be able to know this, and that is the basis for a majority of Parliament's parties, or at least a number of parties representing a majority of the Folketing, to have negotiated a circular, and in the circular we have described the conditions to be met. All the people on this bill are living up to those conditions, otherwise they have had their case dealt with individually in the Committee on Bodily Rights and were granted an exemption. This debate raises two important issues for me. One is the question whether it really is that the Conservative People's Party believes that, even if people live up to those conditions, there must be a political debate on top of this in the Committee on the Rights of the Child where people have to be able to take people off this bill behind a closed door. Is that really the view of the Conservative People's Party? Without a justification and without general principles of administrative law which do not apply in this area because of this constitutional provision, should it be the case that the foreigners living in our country should not be able to access a website and know that if they comply with these conditions, they can obtain citizenship? I think this is an untenable political position, and I regret that the Conservative People's Party seems to have taken that position. The second thing I want to say is that we have reached an agreement. After all, the government, the Left, the Conservative and Liberal Alliance have reached an agreement with the aim that we know that the mandates behind that agreement will also vote through the bill. This will then not have any real political significance, because there is still a large majority coming to vote in favour of this bill, I hope. But why have we actually negotiated with the Conservative People's Party? We've given and taken all of them. Why have we negotiated with the Conservative People's Party if, afterwards, they do not intend to vote through the bill that directly implements that agreement? This raises some parliamentary questions which I would ask the Conservative Parliamentary Group to consider carefully here over Christmas. And now I'm not gonna stand here and ruin Christmas peace, but I actually think it raises some questions. So in those words, I would really like to appeal to us to discuss how strict the rules should be. Social democracy and the government are in favour of strict rules, but it should be so in our country that once we have agreed the rules and the people seek and they live up to it, they must also be sure that they will actually be granted citizenship, with the one small exception, that sometimes we get a message from the police intelligence service, which mentions certain names in confidence to the Committee on Insights, and where we have a tradition in a majority that those names may then risk being tampered with. That is the small exception, but otherwise, in normal circumstances, you will get citizenship if you meet the conditions, and I would urge that the Folketing does not move away from what has been customary for so many years in this Chamber.

Here we agreed that it was important that we did it in Denmark, but that it was also important that we tried to get others to do the same, because if you look at the decision to stop in the North Sea in isolation, it is clear that relative to the total emissions of the world it is perhaps a limited effect, even at a time when there is a certain leakage effect probably associated with that action, but if we can take the lead and we can change it can mean something. This report, written by these NGOs, and thus also the praise to Denmark, is of course a report that has been provided a few days, if not a few weeks before the COP, even though it was published on the COP, and therefore one could not of course know that at the COP this project actually ended up being an even greater success than we had dared to hope for. There was very, very much interest in the press conference at the COP, where we launched the alliance and 11 national and subnational governments chose to join, including Quebec in Canada and Greenland, which, overall, are two units with very, very large oil reserves, and a large number of other important countries. And it was highlighted by many as something that can seriously put pressure on other countries and thus hopefully start a positive spiral. Climate challenges are the greatest challenge of our time. I don't need a lot of references to that, because we all know that. In fact, I think that each of us has our own landmark experience, where we really realised ourselves that the climate challenges are real and serious. I'd like to start my speech by sharing my own experience with you. I grew up with a father who was an electronics technician in the wind turbine industry, so the green transition has always been very close to me. In fact, I've been at major wind fairs in Husum since I was a kid. But the seriousness may not have really dawned on me until the summer of 2018. We had moved a few years before, and one of the first things we did was to build a garden with lawn and lots of flowers and plants. I was in the luggage that I have lived and studied in California outside San Francisco, an area that suffers so much from drought and water supply problems. In law school over there, I had a case about regulating the water supply to get to grips with these problems, and at the same time I was able to walk by well-suited gardens daily with roses and even strawberry plants. I can just tell those of you who have been in the area that you probably know that something like that doesn't grow naturally in such an area... it requires permanent water hoses in the beds, and that requires daily irrigation. It provoked me. And I remember having a conversation with one of my rooms, which came from the east coast of the United States, and he thought that the Californians should simply adapt to their climate. It is not good to plant such a thing, and one must plant according to the climate in which one finds himself, he argued. I thought the same thing about my garden back home in Denmark. So when the heat and drought came in the summer of 2018, I thought: It's gonna be okay. I firmly maintained that my garden is a natural Danish garden; there is no need to water in Denmark. But I got smarter: I've never seen our garden so withered. The brown grass snapped under the feet and was almost nonexistent, and several of the fine flowers and plants never recovered again. For me, this was a very unreal experience and a completely surreal picture of a Danish summer. For farmers in the area, I know it was far worse. Then why am I telling this story? Well, I do, because I think it is important that we remind each other of how serious the climate crisis is and that we already feel it ourselves, even though it may be small here in Denmark. But that makes the big problems so much more real. For example, last week we read a news report that the highest temperature has now been measured ever in the Arctic. Exactly 38 degrees were measured last summer. It sounds crazy. I think that is very good to have as a backdrop for today's debate. This tells us that there is a need to take the climate crisis seriously and that we need to politically push ourselves even more for action, even if we may think that we are already ambitious. It is therefore also irresponsible to ignore the burning platform currently facing climate challenges. I am quite convinced that it will be the greatest attempt of my political generation to bring about a green transition that will be successful. This leads me on to the status of the climate and the government's statement on climate effects for 2021, which is, of course, what we are discussing today. In the government's statement, it does not lag behind in commending words about the government's own climate action. It didn't do that in the speech from Mrs. Anne Paulin just now. The government writes that it concluded almost 50 political agreements on behalf of the climate, that agreements are reached at a high pace, that the government has a very ambitious climate policy, that it is already halfway through to achieving the 70-percent target, etc. That all sounds very good. But there is also a need to wear critical glasses. And as an opposition politician, it is also my duty to criticise the government. So you're gonna have to live with that. The opposition doctrine is that it is too little, it is too late, it is too bad. In fact, I believe that the opposition doctrine has been invented by a Social Democrat in opposition. But, frankly, when it comes to the socialist climate policy, I think that the criticism is so justified. Fine contractual texts and flashy speeches are not enough to bring us into line with the climate challenge. First something about why the government's policy is too little: we recognise that the government has finally presented a roadmap this year on how it intends to achieve the 70-percent target by 2030. But the thing is, with that road map, the government is postponing the problems as far as 2025. According to the roadmap, all the measures that must bring us into line with the 70-percent objective must not be adopted until 2025. And the government doesn't deliver on how we get to the finish line. We need real reductions and investments. That's too little. The same scenarios apply to wind and solar power, which according to the Climate Council will be a major shortage by 2030. A comprehensive plan for the development of renewable energy needs to be put in place quickly. Then, when we come to the 2025 climate target, which is actually in the near future, the government has not described at all what concrete measures should be taken, not even illustrated how we can achieve the milestone. If the sub-target is to be achieved, action by the government is urgently needed. The government has done too little in this respect. So something about why the government's policy is also too late: we have all heard about the hockey staff and the logic that future technologies should achieve us. However, although the government was quick to withdraw it, it seems that the government has resumed its slightly laissez-faire attitude to climate policy. Because if you don't solve the 2025 milestone, we really have a hockey stick. Unfortunately, we are once again witnessing climate change this year. Green tax reform is postponed, green public procurement strategy comes only later to mention just two of the latest examples. It's climate beers, and it's too late. Finally, something about why the government's policy is too bad: In the government's statement you can read: When we can conduct such an ambitious Danish climate policy as we do, this is largely due to our tradition of entering into broad political settlements. The minister actually points out, let me quote, that we work together across the party. We would like that to be the case, but this year, unfortunately, the government has chosen to conclude a narrow agreement on the 2025 climate component, and this is despite the fact that a broad majority in the Folketing is included in the agreement on the Climate Act. And if you think it was a one-time thing, then we got smarter with this year's Finance Act. Here the government has made a narrow agreement with its support parties on the development of offshore winds and CO2 2 That, frankly, is too bad. It was climate criticism, but I would be a little too sorry if my speech contained only concern and criticism. That is why I will conclude by looking forward. Because even if, when it comes to the climate, there is much to worry about, and even if, when it comes to the government's climate policy, there is much to criticise, there is also good reason to be optimistic. As we see it in the Left, Denmark has every chance of being a pioneer on the climate agenda. We have an industry that is so innovative, thinks in green solutions and is adaptable. They have long learned that the green transition is necessary and that it offers plenty of opportunities for a small country like Denmark. They're kind of just waiting for us. That is why we also believe that we need a market-based green transition. It is the best and most effective for the climate. Climate challenges should not be solved by political project-making with various support schemes and regulations all the way up to the 70-percent objective. No, the climate challenges need to be solved through the market, and that is why we would also like to call on the government to come up with an ambitious plan for green tax reform as soon as possible, where CO2 is the only way to become a genuine green pioneer for the rest of the world. With that invitation, I would like to conclude by saying that we are looking forward to • hopefully taking part in several negotiations next year on climate change. And let me remind you that we are a very broad majority in this Parliament who would like to see the green transition. I also think, in all modesty, that we can play a positive and constructive role in these negotiations with good climate solutions. Then I have a proposal for adoption which I would like to present on behalf of the Left, the Conservative People's Party and the Liberal Alliance: proposals for adoption The climate crisis is one of the biggest challenges of our time and requires significant action and interaction with business. Although broad climate agreements have been drawn up, we are far from achieving them. The Danish Parliament recognises that the government has finally presented a roadmap to reach the 70-percent target, but the Danish Parliament considers it critical that the government should therefore also postpone the decisions on the 70-percent target until 2025. In addition, the government does not provide answers to solve 50-54 per cent. reduction in 2025 as adopted by the Folketing. The Folketing therefore instructs the government to present an action plan within the next 6 months on how the government will achieve the 2025 climate component. The Danish Parliament regrets that this year the government has chosen to conclude a narrow agreement with its supporting parties on the 2025 milestone as well as the development of offshore winds and the partial agreement on CCS in the middle of a broad negotiation process on an overall strategy for CCS. One of the main reasons for the ambitious Danish climate policy is our tradition of reaching broad settlements. • V 23).

ParlaMint-DK_20211221222846	The debate is open to the Minister for Employment.
ParlaMint-DK_20211221222912	Thank you. Turning to both the rapporteurs who have found their way to the Chamber, but also to the many viewers who are probably still sitting out there following this important debate, I would like to begin by stating that it is heartblood for the government to safeguard Danish welfare and, of course, the Danish labour market model. The organisation of the Danish welfare society and the Danish labour market is a matter for the Folketing and for the social partners. That is why it is also the government's view that leave rights are best dealt with nationally rather than at EU level. Having said that, the government clearly wants more Danish fathers to take more leave and that Danish leave rules become as flexible as possible. That is why I am also pleased that on 26 October 2021 we reached a broad political agreement on earmarked leave with the Left, SF, Radicals, Enhedslisten and the Alternative. With the political agreement, we are at least implementing the Leaves Directive and earmarking 9 weeks for mother and father respectively. The government does not want to earmark more than the 9 weeks, because it is also important that families have as much flexibility as possible. In this way, the individual family will have the opportunity to adjust to make the best possible sense to them. Because this is an area which, of course, involves a great many families. And just last Thursday, I was also on the platform because we were dealing with a citizen's proposal for specifically earmarked leave. I assume that it is autumn's debate on earmarked leave that has also given rise to the motion for a resolution which we are going to deal with now. With the motion for a resolution, the proposers want to oblige the government to bring up common EU child protection rules at the next EU summit, to start work on identifying the extent to which the EU has the competences and powers to regulate in the field of maternity, and to work towards a possible transfer of competence to the nation states. Let me first state that the European Union has the competence to regulate in this area. In September of this year, the Committee on Employment and Social Affairs, at the request of Mr Giscard d'Estaing, held a meeting of the Committee on Employment and Social Affairs. Morten Messerschmidt asked a written parliamentary question, namely parliamentary question 694 on the same subject, with which the EC Treaty Denmark transferred powers to the EU in the field of maternity. As the current Minister for Employment replied in my absence because I myself was on paternity leave, it is the view that the then Community had the opportunity to regulate the area even when Denmark joined in 1973. Already in the 1970s and 1980s, directives were adopted in the field of employment law on rights by, inter alia, transfers of undertakings, collective redundancies and solvency. Later, the Maastricht Treaty provided a more specific legal basis. Therefore, there is no need to assess whether there is competence, because there is. That also means that we can go straight back to the question of withdrawing competence that the authors of the motion for a resolution are proposing. If the EU's powers in the field of leave are to be withdrawn, it is of course necessary to amend the Treaty. A process of Treaty change is an important event, and it will mean that all Member States and other actors at European level will be able to express their wish for changes and additions, as well as to note changes and additions which may well be contrary to Danish interests. This is not at all a way that we would otherwise need to go down at this stage. The government is sceptical about the EU's ability to regulate large parts of the labour market. We have, for example. in writing. - (DE) We voted against the directive on adequate minimum wages. However, there are also areas where we welcome EU regulation, for example. In the field of health and safety at work, and most recently there is Danish support in the area of equal pay. We believe that the European Union must work to achieve concrete results for its citizens and not throw cooperation into years of discussion on possible treaty changes. Nor should we put ourselves outside the door. We cannot solve the many cross-border challenges alone, for example. and, most importantly, the challenges of social dumping. In this respect, in recent years there have been improvements in EU rules that can contribute to the fight against social dumping, where Denmark has helped to push. This applies, among other things, to: the revised Posting of Workers Directive. After all, the revised Posting of Workers Directive brought the balance of EU rules into line in favour of worker protection. For example: It was made clear that the usual remuneration can be claimed and not just the minimum wage, and a service provider can post a worker for a maximum period of 18 months before the rules of the host country are fully applicable. Good improvements have therefore been made. But the government's fight doesn't stop here. We are among the countries that have pressed for even better opportunities to check that postings are real, including not being posted to mailbox companies. Specifically, we have proposed the introduction of an ID card for migrant and posted workers. At present there is a movement in a number of countries, in the Commission and in the European Parliament, advocating that mobile workers should have a social security passport; especially after the announcements of the new German Government, this case can be given impetus. With countries such as Belgium, the Netherlands, Germany and Austria, Denmark has also addressed the issue of the posting of third-country nationals under the auspices of the European Employment Authority (ELA), so this is now a priority area. In general, it is essential that we use the opportunities offered by EU rules and implement them with respect for our national labour market model. For example: the government, on the basis of the EU rules on posting, together with a broad majority in the Folketing, including the Danish People's Party, has introduced rules to help prevent social dumping on the roads. The examples I have mentioned here show that we can and must work for Danish interests and concrete results within the framework of EU cooperation. We do best to do this by staying at the negotiating table and negotiating in the European Union rather than fighting for new reservations and treaty changes. Having said that, I do not think it is realistic that all countries should agree to such an idea of withdrawing competences. With the comments I have made, the government cannot support the motion for a resolution and, on behalf of the government, I call for the motion for a resolution to be rejected. Thank you for the floor.

I and my party, the Danish People's Party, will by no means welcome that unwelcome.
